CAT-VARC-C10-001 · Para summary · EasyMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

A promise binds us partly because it lets another person plan around what we will do. That is why a promise cannot simply be cancelled when keeping it becomes inconvenient: the inconvenience was foreseeable, and the other person relied on us precisely so that our later moods would not matter. Yet it does not follow that every promise binds in every circumstance. If I promise to meet a friend for lunch and on the way I find a stranger injured on the road, no sensible friend would think I had wronged them by stopping. What releases me is not my changed preference but a change the promise was never meant to cover, one that both of us, had we thought of it, would have agreed to exclude. The force of a promise, then, is real but bounded by what it was understood to be about.

  1. (A)Because other people plan around what we have promised them, a promise must be kept whatever happens, and only the person to whom it was made can release us from it.
  2. (B)Stopping to help an injured stranger on the way to a lunch appointment is not a wrong against the friend who is kept waiting at the table.
  3. (C)A promise binds only for as long as the person who made it still wishes to keep it, since a change of preference is itself a change of circumstances.
  4. (D)A promise holds against mere inconvenience because others rely on it, but it lapses when circumstances arise that both parties would have agreed it was never meant to cover.
CAT-VARC-C10-002 · Para summary · MediumMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

Two pictures of moral worth pull in different directions. On one, the person who is honest only by overcoming a strong temptation to lie deserves more credit than the person to whom lying never occurs, since the first has had to do something and the second has merely been lucky in temperament. On the other, the person who must struggle reveals that part of them still wants the wrong thing, while the person for whom honesty is second nature shows what a good character actually looks like. Each picture captures something. The struggler's effort is admirable as an achievement, but we would not wish our friends to remain strugglers for ever; we hope that effort will eventually turn into ease. Effort, on this view, is the honourable path to virtue rather than its finest form.

  1. (A)Overcoming temptation deserves admiration as an achievement, but the ideal is a character for which doing right has become natural, so effort is a route to virtue rather than its peak.
  2. (B)Since each picture of moral worth captures something true, neither can be preferred to the other, and moral credit must always be judged case by case.
  3. (C)A person who is honest without any effort deserves little moral credit, since an untroubled temperament is a matter of luck, whereas the struggler has actually had to achieve something difficult.
  4. (D)Struggling against temptation shows that a person's character is defective, so effortful honesty has no real moral value when it is compared with natural honesty.
CAT-VARC-C10-003 · Para summary · DifficultMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

Moral philosophers often test principles against imagined cases: a runaway trolley, a surgeon with five dying patients, a drowning child beside an expensive suit. The method is defended on the ground that stripping away messy detail isolates the feature that matters. But the stripping is not neutral. A real surgeon could never be certain that the transplant would succeed, that no one would find out, or that the healthy visitor had no one depending on him; our reluctance to approve the killing may be a sound response to exactly these uncertainties, which the case asks us to set aside but which our intuitions were formed to track. When an intuition about an idealised case conflicts with a principle, it is therefore unclear which has been refuted. This does not make imagined cases useless, since they can still show that a principle has implications its defenders had not noticed; it makes them weaker as evidence against principles than they are usually taken to be.

  1. (A)Because imagined cases strip away the uncertainties that shape real moral judgement, they cannot tell us anything reliable about moral principles and ought to be abandoned by philosophers.
  2. (B)Our reluctance to approve of a surgeon killing one visitor to save five patients is best explained by our doubts about whether the transplant would really succeed.
  3. (C)Intuitions about stripped-down cases may still track real features the case removes, so such cases can expose unnoticed implications of principles but are weaker evidence against them than assumed.
  4. (D)When an intuition about an idealised case clashes with a moral principle, the intuition should be distrusted and the principle retained, because the imagined case is too unrealistic to count.
CAT-VARC-C10-004 · Para summary · MediumMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

It is sometimes objected that an institution cannot meaningfully apologise for wrongs committed before any of its present members were born, since none of them did anything wrong. The objection assumes that an apology is mainly a confession of personal guilt. But an apology also does something else: it states publicly that what happened was a wrong, not a misfortune or a regrettable necessity, and it commits the speaker to a relationship in which that wrong is not repeated or excused. A university, a company or a city persists across generations precisely as the bearer of such commitments; it signs contracts that outlive its staff and honours debts its founders incurred. If it can inherit obligations of this kind, it can also inherit the duty to acknowledge what was done in its name. What it cannot do is make its present members feel personal guilt, and an apology that demands such feeling misunderstands its own purpose.

  1. (A)Universities, companies and cities ought to pay compensation to the descendants of those they wronged, in the same way that they repay the debts their founders incurred.
  2. (B)Because an apology also names a wrong publicly and commits to a changed relationship, an enduring institution can apologise for past wrongs, though not impose personal guilt on present members.
  3. (C)Institutions cannot sincerely apologise for wrongs done before their current members were born, because an apology is above all a confession of personal guilt by the person who committed the wrong.
  4. (D)An institutional apology succeeds only when it makes the present members of the institution feel personally guilty for what was once done in its name.
CAT-VARC-C10-005 · Para summary · EasyMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

Nostalgia was once treated as a kind of illness, a homesickness that weakened soldiers and emigrants. A more recent view sees it as doing useful work. People tend to become nostalgic when they feel lonely, uncertain or adrift, and the memories they return to are usually of moments shared with others: a family meal, a school trip, a celebration. Recalling such moments reminds a person that they have been valued and connected, and that sense of continuity can steady them in an unsettled present. Nostalgia is not always benign; it can harden into a refusal to accept change, or into a fantasy of a past that never was. But in its ordinary form it seems less a flight from the present than a way of drawing on the past to cope with it.

  1. (A)People who feel lonely or uncertain are more likely than others to recall the family meals, school trips and celebrations they once shared.
  2. (B)Nostalgia is essentially a retreat from the present, a form of homesickness that leaves people less able to deal with the difficult circumstances they face.
  3. (C)Because it recalls moments of connection, nostalgia is always beneficial, and fears that it encourages resistance to change are without foundation.
  4. (D)Ordinary nostalgia, though it can turn into denial of change or a fantasy past, mostly helps people cope with an unsettled present by recalling times of connection.
CAT-VARC-C10-006 · Para summary · MediumMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

When a decision turns out badly, observers tend to judge it as having been a poor decision, even when they are told exactly what the decision-maker knew at the time. A doctor whose reasonable choice of treatment is followed by a rare complication is rated as less competent than one who made the same choice and was lucky. This tendency to grade decisions by their outcomes is often described simply as an error, and in many settings it is one. Yet it is not wholly irrational. In daily life we rarely know what someone knew, and outcomes are often the only evidence we have of their judgement; a rule of thumb that trusts outcomes is a sensible response to that ignorance. The difficulty is that we go on applying the rule even when the facts that would make it unnecessary are placed in front of us.

  1. (A)Grading decisions by results makes sense when we cannot tell what the decision-maker knew, but people keep doing it even once they have been told.
  2. (B)Judging a decision by its outcome is an irrational error that observers should drop entirely, since outcomes reveal nothing about the quality of a person's choice.
  3. (C)Doctors whose patients suffer rare complications are unfairly rated as less competent than equally careful colleagues who simply happened to be lucky with their patients.
  4. (D)Because outcomes are usually the best evidence of judgement, people are right to grade decisions by results even when they know what the decision-maker knew.
CAT-VARC-C10-007 · Para summary · DifficultMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

Ask people why they chose one pair of socks over another, why they liked a job candidate, why they moved house, and they will readily give reasons. It is tempting to treat these reasons as reports, read off from some inner record of what actually moved them. There is good ground for doubt. People's explanations often match what an outsider would guess, given the culture's ordinary theories of why people act, more closely than they match whatever in fact influenced the choice; and when an influence was something they did not notice, such as the position of an item on a shelf, they seldom mention it. It does not follow that people know nothing of their own minds. They are usually right about what they feel and what they are attending to. What is shaky is their access to the causes of their choices, which they seem to infer much as anyone else would, while experiencing the inference as direct knowledge.

  1. (A)People have no reliable knowledge of their own minds, since the explanations they offer for their choices merely repeat what any outsider would have guessed about them.
  2. (B)People knowingly give false reasons for their choices, preferring explanations that fit their culture's theories of action to admitting influences they would rather hide.
  3. (C)People generally know what they feel and attend to, but their accounts of why they chose something seem to be inferences from common theories of behaviour, mistakenly felt as direct insight.
  4. (D)People's explanations of their choices tend to leave out influences they did not notice, such as where an item stood on a shelf, and so are incomplete rather than mistaken.
CAT-VARC-C10-008 · Para summary · MediumMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

Procrastination is usually treated as a failure of time management, and the usual remedies follow from that diagnosis: calendars, lists, deadlines broken into smaller pieces. These help some people, but they often fail the chronic procrastinator, who typically knows perfectly well how much time remains and what needs to be done. A better account starts from feeling rather than scheduling. The task that is put off is usually one that provokes some discomfort, whether boredom, self-doubt or fear of being judged, and delay offers immediate relief from that discomfort at the cost of greater trouble later. Seen this way, the procrastinator is not miscalculating time but managing a mood, badly. Planning tools are not useless, but they work best when they also make the task feel less threatening, for instance by turning a vague, daunting assignment into a first step small enough to begin without dread.

  1. (A)Calendars, lists and deadlines are useless against procrastination, which can be cured only by tackling the fear of being judged that lies behind every task a person keeps delaying.
  2. (B)Chronic procrastination is less poor time management than an escape from a task's unpleasant feelings, so planning aids help mainly when they also make the task feel less threatening.
  3. (C)Procrastinators put off tasks because they underestimate how long the work will take, so breaking deadlines into smaller pieces is the most effective remedy available to them.
  4. (D)Procrastinators deliberately trade present relief for later trouble after calmly weighing the two, which shows that their habit of delay is a rational choice.
CAT-VARC-C10-009 · Para summary · EasyMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

It is often said that public libraries have lost their purpose now that so much information can be found online. The argument mistakes what libraries do. Lending books was always only part of their role; they are also among the few indoor public places where a person may stay for hours without buying anything, where a student without a quiet room at home can study, an elderly reader can find company, and a job seeker can use a computer and ask for help. These functions have not moved online, and for people with little money or space they may matter more than ever. Libraries certainly need to change what they offer, but the case for keeping them does not rest on books alone.

  1. (A)Students who lack a quiet room at home depend on public libraries as a place where they can study undisturbed for long hours.
  2. (B)Public libraries should stop lending books altogether and turn themselves entirely into community centres that offer computers, help and social activities.
  3. (C)Even with information online, libraries still matter because they also provide free space, room to study, company and practical help that poorer people cannot easily find elsewhere.
  4. (D)Now that information is freely available on the internet, public libraries have lost most of their purpose and need no longer be maintained by towns and cities.
CAT-VARC-C10-010 · Para summary · MediumMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

Jokes are notoriously the hardest thing to translate, and the reason is not simply that they rely on puns. Even a joke with no wordplay depends on what its audience already knows and expects: which professions are thought pompous, which towns are thought backward, which subjects are too serious to mention lightly. The joke works by briefly upsetting these expectations, and a listener who does not share them sees nothing upset. A translator can explain the background, but an explained joke has lost the speed on which its effect depends. The more faithful strategy is often to replace the joke with a different one that does similar work for the new audience, accepting that fidelity to the effect may require infidelity to the words.

  1. (A)Jokes are hard to translate mainly because puns depend on the sounds and double meanings of words in one language, which rarely survive the move into another.
  2. (B)No text of any kind can be translated faithfully, because every piece of writing depends on assumptions that are shared only by its original audience.
  3. (C)A good translator of humour should add brief notes explaining the cultural background of each joke, since readers from another culture cannot otherwise see why it is funny.
  4. (D)Humour resists translation because it plays on an audience's shared expectations, which explanation cannot supply without killing the joke, so translators may need to substitute new jokes.
CAT-VARC-C10-011 · Para summary · MediumMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

Two attitudes have long divided those who care for old buildings. One holds that a ruined church or fort should be restored to the state its builders intended, replacing lost towers and repainting faded walls, so that visitors see the work as it was meant to be seen. The other holds that such restoration is a kind of forgery: the building's age, its damage and its later alterations are part of its history, and the honest course is to stop further decay while leaving the marks of time visible. The second attitude has largely prevailed among conservation specialists, and with reason, since reconstruction often rests on guesswork about what the original looked like. Yet it is not costless. A building conserved as a fragment may be honest but unintelligible to visitors who cannot picture the whole, and some careful, clearly marked reconstruction may be the price of making the past legible.

  1. (A)Conserving old buildings as found is usually more honest than restoring them, though some limited and clearly marked reconstruction may be needed so that visitors can understand the whole.
  2. (B)Restoring old buildings to their original appearance is a form of forgery and should never be attempted, since any reconstruction of a lost part rests on guesswork.
  3. (C)Specialists disagree about whether to restore or to conserve old buildings, and since each approach has real costs as well as benefits, neither can be preferred as a general rule.
  4. (D)Old buildings should be restored to the state their builders intended, because visitors cannot appreciate a structure that has been left standing as a ruined fragment.
CAT-VARC-C10-012 · Para summary · DifficultMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

Collectors who travelled villages writing down folk songs are usually praised for saving a heritage that would otherwise have vanished. The praise is deserved, but it hides a paradox. A song in oral circulation has no single correct version: each singer alters the words, the tune and the length to suit the occasion, and this variation is not corruption but the way the tradition lives. Once a collector prints one version, often the one sung by the most cooperative informant on a particular afternoon, that version tends to become the standard, taught in schools and performed at festivals, while the others come to seem like mistakes. The act of preservation thus fixes what was fluid. None of this means collectors should have stayed at home; without them most of these songs would be lost entirely. It means that a printed collection should be read as a snapshot of a moving practice, not as the authentic original from which variants departed.

  1. (A)Folk song collectors did more harm than good to the traditions they studied, since printing a single version of each song destroyed the variation that kept them alive.
  2. (B)Collecting folk songs saved them, but making one often arbitrary version the standard hid the variation that was the tradition itself, so printed texts should be read as snapshots, not originals.
  3. (C)Collectors of folk songs ought to have printed every variant they heard in a village, because a single version cannot fairly represent a tradition that changes with each singer.
  4. (D)Variation among singers gradually corrupted folk songs, so collectors were right to fix the most authentic version of each song in print before it was lost.
CAT-VARC-C10-013 · Para summary · EasyMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

Historians value memoirs for their vividness but treat them with caution, and the reasons are not mainly about honesty. A memoir is usually written long after the events, when the author knows how things turned out, and memory tends to reshape the past to fit that knowledge: doubts are forgotten, lucky guesses become foresight, and decisions made in confusion acquire a clear purpose. Memoirists also write for an audience and often to defend a reputation. None of this makes memoirs worthless. They record impressions, motives and atmosphere that official papers omit, and read alongside letters and documents from the time, they can be revealing. The caution is about using them alone as a record of what happened and why.

  1. (A)Because memoirs are shaped by hindsight and the wish to persuade, historians value their vivid detail but test them against records from the time instead of relying on them alone.
  2. (B)Historians distrust memoirs chiefly because their authors usually lie on purpose, inventing events in order to protect their own reputations from the criticism of later writers.
  3. (C)Memoirs written long after the events they describe tend to present lucky guesses as foresight and confused decisions as having had a clear purpose from the start.
  4. (D)Since memory reshapes the past to fit what came later, memoirs are worthless as historical evidence and should be ignored in favour of official papers written at the time.
CAT-VARC-C10-014 · Para summary · MediumMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

Counterfactual history, the study of what might have happened had some event gone differently, is often dismissed as a parlour game for historians with too much time. The dismissal overlooks that every causal claim in history is already a counterfactual. To say that a drought caused a rebellion is to say that without the drought the rebellion would not have occurred, or would have come later or smaller; a historian who refuses to think about alternatives cannot weigh causes at all. The real objection is to counterfactuals that stray far from the moment in question, imagining chains of consequences decades long, where every step multiplies uncertainty. Asking what was genuinely possible for the people involved, given what they knew and could do, is not fantasy but part of explaining why events took the course they did.

  1. (A)Counterfactual history is an idle pastime, because what did not happen cannot be studied and so tells us nothing about the real causes of events that did happen.
  2. (B)Since every causal claim involves a counterfactual, historians should freely imagine how the world would have developed over many decades had key events gone otherwise.
  3. (C)A historian who claims that a drought caused a rebellion is implicitly committed to the view that without the drought the rebellion would not have happened as it did.
  4. (D)Thinking about alternatives is unavoidable in assigning historical causes and is legitimate when kept to options open at the time; only long chains of imagined consequences deserve dismissal.
CAT-VARC-C10-015 · Para summary · MediumMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

We tend to think of a map as a picture of what is already there, accurate or inaccurate according to how faithfully it copies the land. Many maps in history did more than copy. When a state surveyed a frontier region and drew a line across it, the line often did not record a boundary that people on the ground recognised; it created one, which taxes, passports and armies would later enforce. Rivers that had joined communities became borders that separated them. To say this is not to deny that maps can be more or less accurate about coastlines and mountains. It is to recognise that for things like boundaries, ownership and jurisdiction, the map was frequently an instrument for making the world rather than a record of it.

  1. (A)When states surveyed and mapped frontier regions, rivers that had once connected neighbouring communities were often turned into borders that divided them, later enforced with taxes and passports.
  2. (B)Maps may copy physical features well or badly, but for boundaries and jurisdiction they often created the divisions they seemed only to record.
  3. (C)Historical maps of frontier regions were frequently inaccurate, because the surveyors sent by states misrecorded the boundaries that local people already recognised on the ground.
  4. (D)Maps are never faithful copies of the land, since every map, including those of coastlines and mountains, reflects the political aims of the state that commissioned it.
CAT-VARC-C10-016 · Para summary · DifficultMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

Historians have learned to read archives not only for what they say but for what they leave out. An archive is the residue of an administration's interests: it keeps tax rolls, court cases and correspondence among officials, and so it records people mainly when they paid, offended or petitioned. Whole areas of life, such as household work, friendship and private belief, appear only when they collided with authority. It has become common to conclude that the archive simply silences the powerless. That conclusion goes too far. The same court records that reduce a person to a defendant often preserve, in the words of witnesses, details of daily life recorded nowhere else, and a petition written to flatter an official can still reveal what its writer wanted. The archive is biased in what it selects, but read against its grain it yields more about ordinary lives than its makers ever intended.

  1. (A)Because archives keep only what served an administration's interests, they silence the powerless, and their records cannot tell historians much about ordinary people's lives.
  2. (B)Court records are more useful than petitions to historians of daily life, because witnesses in court speak more freely than people who are writing in order to flatter an official.
  3. (C)Archives record ordinary people mainly where they met authority, yet saying they simply silence the powerless goes too far, since records read against their purpose reveal much about daily life.
  4. (D)Archives are far less biased than historians have come to assume, since court records and petitions represent ordinary people as fully as they represent officials.
CAT-VARC-C10-017 · Para summary · EasyMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

Loyalty programmes look like a gift: buy nine coffees and the tenth is free. For the seller, the point is less the discount than the switching cost it creates. A customer who is halfway to a reward has a reason to return to the same shop even when another is closer or cheaper, because going elsewhere means abandoning the progress already made. The programme also gives the seller information about what customers buy and how often, which can be used to target offers. Customers are not necessarily fooled; many value the rewards and would shop in the same place anyway. But the programme is designed mainly to make leaving a little more expensive than staying.

  1. (A)Loyalty programmes are mainly a way for shops to reward their regular customers with discounts, out of gratitude for their continued custom over time.
  2. (B)Loyalty programmes trick customers into paying more than they otherwise would, so shoppers who join them always end up worse off than those who do not.
  3. (C)Shops use the information collected through loyalty cards to find out what their customers buy and how often, and then send them targeted offers.
  4. (D)Loyalty schemes chiefly serve sellers by making it costlier for customers to switch, while also gathering purchase data, even if many customers benefit from them too.
CAT-VARC-C10-018 · Para summary · MediumMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

A price of zero is not just a very low price. People respond to free offers far more strongly than a small drop in price would predict: a product reduced from a small sum to nothing attracts many more takers than one reduced by the same amount to a slightly smaller sum. One reason is that a free choice seems to carry no possible loss, so the usual weighing of costs and benefits is skipped. This is why many online services charge nothing and earn their revenue from advertising or data instead. The user does pay, in attention and in information about themselves, but these costs are spread out and hard to count, and so they do not trigger the caution that a visible price, however small, would.

  1. (A)Governments ought to require online services to charge a small but visible price, so that users would weigh the hidden costs of their attention and data more carefully.
  2. (B)Free offers draw people disproportionately because zero seems to carry no risk of loss, letting services charge in attention and data, real costs too diffuse to prompt caution.
  3. (C)Online services that charge nothing are genuinely free to their users, since what users hand over in exchange, such as their attention, has no real value to them.
  4. (D)A product whose price is cut from a small sum to nothing gains many more buyers than one whose price is cut by the same amount but stays above zero.
CAT-VARC-C10-019 · Para summary · MediumMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

Open-plan offices were promoted on the promise that removing walls would bring colleagues together and multiply the chance conversations from which ideas emerge. When companies have tracked what actually happens after such a move, the picture has often been the reverse: people conscious of being overheard send more messages and speak less face to face, wearing headphones to create the privacy that walls once gave. It would be wrong, however, to conclude that open plans always reduce collaboration. Teams whose work requires constant quick coordination, such as a newsroom or a trading floor, may genuinely benefit. The lesson is that physical closeness does not by itself produce interaction; whether it does depends on what the work requires and on whether people feel free to talk without being observed.

  1. (A)Open plans often cut face-to-face talk, though not for every team, since closeness yields interaction only where work demands it and people feel unobserved.
  2. (B)Bringing colleagues physically closer together reliably multiplies the chance conversations from which ideas emerge, which is why open plans suit most companies better than walled offices.
  3. (C)Newsrooms and trading floors gain from open-plan layouts because their teams need to coordinate constantly and quickly, unlike most office workers, who would prefer walls.
  4. (D)Open-plan offices always reduce collaboration among colleagues, because employees who fear being overheard retreat behind headphones and communicate only through written messages.
CAT-VARC-C10-020 · Para summary · EasyMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

In many markets, small traders lend goods to one another on credit without any written contract, and the arrangement works far better than an outsider might expect. The reason is that these traders deal with each other repeatedly and talk constantly. A trader who fails to repay does not merely lose one partner; word spreads, and soon no one in the market will extend credit to them. The threat of losing that reputation enforces the debt more cheaply than a court could. The system has limits. It works only within a community dense enough for news to travel, and it tends to exclude newcomers, who have no reputation to lose and therefore little to offer as security.

  1. (A)Because they have no bad record behind them, newcomers to a market find it easier than established traders to obtain goods on credit without a contract.
  2. (B)A trader who fails to repay a debt in a close-knit market soon finds that no other trader there is willing to sell goods to them on credit.
  3. (C)Informal credit among traders works because repeated dealing and gossip make a lost reputation a strong penalty, but it holds only in close-knit groups and tends to shut out newcomers.
  4. (D)Informal credit based on reputation is cheaper and more reliable than legal contracts, and so it should replace the courts as the main way of enforcing debts everywhere.
CAT-VARC-C10-021 · Para summary · DifficultMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

National income accounts leave out the cooking, cleaning and care done unpaid within households, and the omission is often called a simple bias that a better statistic would correct. The criticism is right that something valuable goes unmeasured, and that this can distort judgements: when a family starts paying for childcare it once provided itself, measured output rises though little more care may be given. But the remedy is less simple than it sounds. To add household work to the accounts one must price it, and every method of pricing an hour of unpaid care, whether at what a hired carer would earn or at what the carer could have earned elsewhere, imposes a market valuation on activity whose meaning to those who do it is often precisely that it is not done for money. The omission is a real flaw, but the obvious fix may trade one distortion for another.

  1. (A)Leaving unpaid household work out of national income is a genuine and misleading flaw, yet including it would require market prices that misrepresent such work, so the fix may bring a distortion of its own.
  2. (B)Unpaid household work cannot be valued in money at all, so national income accounts are right to leave it out, and the critics of the omission are simply mistaken.
  3. (C)When families begin to pay for childcare they previously provided themselves, measured national output rises even though little additional care may actually be given to the children.
  4. (D)Leaving out unpaid household work is a simple bias in national income figures, which statisticians could correct by pricing each hour at what a hired carer would earn.
CAT-VARC-C10-022 · Para summary · EasyMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

Any species that arrives in a new region with human help is now often labelled invasive and treated as a threat to be removed. Some newcomers deserve that reputation: a predator introduced to an island can wipe out birds that evolved without defences against it. But many introduced plants and animals settle in without causing measurable harm, and some come to fill roles left empty by the loss of native species, providing food for pollinators or shelter for birds. Judging a species by its origin rather than its effects can waste money on removing harmless arrivals while drawing attention from the damaging ones. The useful question is not where a species came from but what it actually does.

  1. (A)Because they arrive with human help rather than naturally, introduced species should be removed as soon as they are found in a new region.
  2. (B)Introduced species should be judged by their effects rather than their origin, since while some are destructive, many are harmless or even useful, and blanket removal wastes effort.
  3. (C)Introduced species are generally good for the regions they reach, so efforts to remove them do more harm than good and should be given up altogether.
  4. (D)Predators that people introduce to remote islands can wipe out native birds that evolved without any means of defending themselves against such attackers.
CAT-VARC-C10-023 · Para summary · EasyMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

Journals are more likely to publish studies that find an effect than studies that find none, and researchers, knowing this, are less likely to write up their null results at all. Each choice seems reasonable, since a finding that nothing happened looks less interesting than a discovery. Together, however, they distort the record. If twenty teams test a useless remedy and one obtains a positive result by chance, that one study may be the only one anyone reads, and the remedy will appear to work. The problem lies not in any single dishonest scientist but in a system that filters results by whether they are exciting rather than by whether they are sound.

  1. (A)When many research teams test a useless remedy, one of them is likely to obtain a positive result purely by chance, and that study may be the one that is widely read.
  2. (B)Scientists frequently falsify their data in order to obtain positive results, since journals refuse to publish any study that fails to find an effect for its treatment.
  3. (C)Because journals and researchers both favour positive findings over null ones, the published record can make useless treatments look effective, a fault of the system rather than of dishonest individuals.
  4. (D)Journals should be required by law to publish every study submitted to them, whatever its results, so that null findings are no longer lost to other researchers.

Two strategies compete for the limited money available to protect plant diversity. Seed banks store frozen samples of thousands of species in secure vaults, offering insurance against extinction at modest cost. Conservation in the wild protects plants where they grow, along with the insects, fungi and soils they depend on. The seed bank's great advantage is security, but its samples are frozen in time: a population kept in a vault stops adapting to changing climate and pests, and some species have seeds that cannot survive drying and freezing at all. Wild populations go on evolving, but they are exposed to every threat the vault avoids. Neither strategy can substitute for the other. The vault is best understood as a backup for living populations, not a replacement for them.

  1. (A)Because frozen seeds stop adapting and some cannot be stored at all, money spent on seed banks would be better spent entirely on protecting wild habitats instead.
  2. (B)Since seed banks protect their samples from every threat at modest cost, they are a more efficient way to safeguard plant diversity than conservation in the wild.
  3. (C)Some plant species produce seeds that cannot survive being dried and frozen, so these species cannot be kept in seed banks and must be conserved in the wild.
  4. (D)Seed banks are secure but halt adaptation and miss some species, while wild populations evolve but stay exposed, so vaults should back up, not replace, wild conservation.
CAT-VARC-C10-025 · Para summary · MediumMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

Naturalists have long divided into those who tend to lump similar organisms into one broad species and those who split them into many narrow ones. The dispute is often presented as a quarrel of temperaments, as if one side were simply careless and the other fussy. In fact it reflects something real about living things. Populations diverge gradually, and at any moment some pairs are clearly distinct, some clearly the same, and many lie in between, interbreeding a little or differing in a few traits. For these intermediate cases no fact of nature settles whether there are one species or two; the answer depends on which criterion one adopts. This does not make classification arbitrary, since the clear cases are clear. But it means that some disputes about species are disagreements about definitions rather than about the organisms.

  1. (A)Because populations diverge gradually, many borderline species cases depend on the criterion chosen, so some lumper and splitter disputes concern definitions, though clear cases prevent arbitrariness.
  2. (B)Since populations diverge gradually, the division of living things into species is entirely arbitrary and reflects only the preferences of the naturalists who classify them.
  3. (C)Naturalists who lump organisms into broad species tend to be careless, while those who split them into many narrow species are excessively fussy about small differences.
  4. (D)Disagreements between lumpers and splitters could be settled if naturalists studied the disputed organisms more closely and gathered more evidence about how often and how freely they interbreed.
CAT-VARC-C10-026 · Para summary · MediumMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

Projects that recruit volunteers to count birds, record flowering dates or photograph insects are often dismissed by professional researchers on the grounds that amateurs make mistakes. The objection is true and largely beside the point. Individual records from volunteers are indeed less reliable than those of trained surveyors, but the errors tend to be random rather than systematic, and random errors shrink in importance as the number of observations grows. A thousand imperfect counts spread across a region can reveal a trend that a handful of expert surveys, confined to a few accessible sites, would miss altogether. The real weakness of such data lies elsewhere: volunteers go where it is pleasant to go, at weekends and in good weather, so the gaps in their coverage are anything but random. The question to ask of citizen science is therefore not how careful each participant is but where and when the participants chose to look.

  1. (A)Because the errors volunteers make are random, large citizen science projects produce records more trustworthy than professional surveys, and they should replace such surveys.
  2. (B)Since volunteers survey pleasant places at convenient times, citizen science records reveal more about the leisure habits of people than about birds, plants or insects.
  3. (C)Volunteer records suffer less from individual error, which large numbers offset, than from uneven coverage of the places and times volunteers choose to survey.
  4. (D)Citizen science records are unreliable because amateurs make frequent identification mistakes, and these mistakes become more serious as the number of volunteers grows.

Advocates of rewilding sometimes speak as though removing fences and reintroducing a few large animals will return a landscape to the condition it was in before human interference. The promise is appealing but confused. There is no single earlier condition to return to: the landscapes in question have changed continuously for thousands of years, with shifts in climate, in the species present and in the ways people used them. Choosing a baseline is therefore a decision about which past to prefer, not a discovery of the true one. This does not make rewilding pointless. Letting natural processes such as flooding, grazing and decay operate with less management can produce richer and more resilient ecosystems. But the honest case for it is about the processes it allows, not about a lost original it claims to restore.

  1. (A)Rewilding succeeds best when planners first establish the true condition of a landscape before human interference and then reintroduce the animals that lived there.
  2. (B)Rewilding may be worth doing for the processes it frees, but promising to restore an original landscape misleads, since any baseline is a chosen past.
  3. (C)Because landscapes have always been changing, rewilding has no coherent goal at all, and its supporters cannot justify it on ecological or on any other grounds.
  4. (D)Over thousands of years, shifts in climate, in the species present and in human uses of the land have kept altering the landscapes that rewilding now targets.

An offset that pays to plant a forest is often treated as cancelling an equivalent quantity of emissions from a flight or a factory. The equivalence is only arithmetical. Carbon released from burning fossil fuel stays in the atmosphere for centuries, while carbon stored in trees is held only as long as the trees survive fire, disease, drought and the decisions of future landowners. The two quantities are measured in the same tonnes, yet one is a near-permanent addition and the other a reversible loan. None of this means that planting forests is worthless; a stored tonne buys time, and time has value. It means that an offset should be counted as a delay whose worth depends on how securely the storage can be guaranteed, rather than as an erasure that lets the original emission be forgotten.

  1. (A)Forest offsets are valuable chiefly because they buy time, so a tonne of carbon stored in trees should be counted as equal to a tonne released by burning fuel.
  2. (B)The weakness of forest offsets lies in measurement, since the carbon held in trees cannot be counted as accurately as the carbon released when fuel is burned.
  3. (C)Because trees can burn or be felled, forest offsets do nothing to address emissions, and they should be abandoned in favour of directly cutting the use of fossil fuel.
  4. (D)Because forest storage is reversible while fossil carbon persists, an offset is a delay whose value depends on secure storage, not a cancellation of the emission.

Much of what biologists know about how genes, cells and nervous systems work comes from a small number of species: a fruit fly, a roundworm, a small weed of the mustard family, the laboratory mouse. These organisms were not chosen because they are typical of life. They were chosen because they breed quickly, survive in captivity and can be manipulated easily, and once a community of researchers had built tools and stocks around them, each new study was cheaper to do in the same species than in a new one. The result is a body of knowledge that is deep but narrow, and whose generality is often assumed rather than tested. Findings that hold in a mouse may hold in people, and frequently do; but the reasons the mouse was studied had little to do with its resemblance to us.

  1. (A)Because model organisms were selected for convenience rather than typicality, findings from them rarely apply to people, and medical research should turn to other species.

  4. (D)Model organisms were selected because they represent living things in general, which is why discoveries made in them so often extend to human beings.

Writing tools that suggest the next word or complete a sentence are usually judged by whether their suggestions are accurate, that is, whether they offer what the writer was about to type. By that standard they are improving quickly. But accuracy measured this way rewards the most probable continuation, and the most probable continuation is, by definition, the one that most writers have already used. A tool that is perfectly accurate in this sense would steer each writer a little towards the common phrase and away from the unusual one, and across millions of documents these small nudges could make prose more uniform without anyone deciding that it should be. The danger lies not in any single suggestion, which the writer is free to refuse, but in the cumulative pull of accepting the easy option many times a day.

  1. (A)Writers ought to switch off predictive suggestions altogether, since every suggestion forces common phrases on them and takes away their freedom to choose their own words.

  3. (C)Predictive writing tools have become accurate enough to anticipate what most writers were about to type, which shows how rapidly the technology is now improving.
  4. (D)Since predictive writing tools favour the most common continuation, their cumulative pull could make prose more uniform, even though each single suggestion is easy enough to refuse.

It is often assumed that digital records, because they can be copied without loss, are safer than paper ones. A manuscript can burn; a file can be duplicated a thousand times. But a copy is only useful if something can still read it. Files depend on formats, formats on software, and software on machines and operating systems that are replaced every few years, so a perfectly preserved sequence of bits can become unreadable simply because the programs that understood it have gone. Paper asks nothing of its reader except literacy and light. Digital preservation is therefore less a matter of storing data than of continually maintaining the conditions under which data can be interpreted, a task that never finishes and is easy to neglect.

  4. (D)The main threat to digital archives is that storage hardware wears out quickly, so institutions have to keep buying new disks to hold their collections of files.

The claim assumes that preferences exist fully formed before the recommendations arrive. Taste, however, is partly made by exposure: people come to like music, food or films they have encountered repeatedly, and they rarely develop a liking for what they never meet. A system that learns from what someone has chosen, and then shows them more of the same, is therefore not simply reflecting a preference but helping to fix it, narrowing the range of encounters from which future preferences could grow. This need not be sinister, and the user remains free to search elsewhere. But it means the defence of such systems as neutral mirrors is mistaken.

  3. (C)Recommendation systems serve users well by supplying more of what they already like, though people should still search elsewhere if they wish to broaden their tastes.
  4. (D)Because people come to like what they meet repeatedly, recommendation systems are now the main force that decides which music, food and films people enjoy.

When a writer dies leaving a novel unfinished, or instructs that drafts be destroyed, those who hold the papers face a familiar dilemma. Publishing seems to betray the author's wishes or, at least, to present as a book what the author never judged to be one. Suppressing seems to deprive readers of work that may be among the best the writer produced. The dilemma is usually framed as loyalty against value, but this framing overlooks a third consideration: how the work is presented. An unfinished draft printed as a draft, with its gaps visible and its status made plain, misrepresents the author far less than the same text smoothed by an editor into the appearance of completion.   1. (A)The fate of a dead writer's unfinished work involves more than loyalty against value; whether it is printed openly as a draft also matters.

Sceptics take this as proof that the art world values names rather than pictures, since the canvas itself has not changed. The inference is too quick. A forger who copies a style after it has been invented has not achieved what the original painter achieved, even if the surfaces are indistinguishable. Some of the lost esteem is snobbery, no doubt; but some of it tracks a real difference that the eye alone cannot see.

  4. (D)A forgery that cannot be told apart from an original has exactly the same artistic worth, since the value of a painting lies wholly in what the eye can see.

It is better understood as a setting with very strong effects of its own. Newspaper headlines have long had a grammar of their own: articles and linking verbs drop out, short words such as 'bid', 'probe' and 'row' replace longer ones, and the present tense is used for events that are already over. These habits arose from a practical constraint, the need to fit a message into a narrow column in large type. They persist on screens, where space is less tight, partly because readers have learned to decode them and partly because brevity still helps a headline compete for attention. The cost is ambiguity: a compressed headline can sometimes be read in two ways, and the reader must open the story to find out which was meant.

The charge contains a truth: children who are told that their way of speaking is wrong often come to feel that it is, and may abandon it. But the fault lies in the telling, not in the teaching of the standard itself. One is that it is unfair: families who can pay for it buy their children an advantage that others cannot. The other is that it defeats the purpose of the test, which was meant to measure ability, by inflating scores without improving the underlying skills. The two objections pull in different directions. If coaching genuinely builds skills, the second objection fails, but the first becomes stronger, since a real advantage is being bought. If coaching merely teaches tricks, the second objection holds, but the unfairness is less troubling, because the advantage it buys is partly illusory. Critics who press both at full strength at once owe an account of how coaching can be both useless and decisive.

  1. (A)Exam coaching is unfair because only families who can afford it gain an advantage, and it should therefore be restricted or else made freely available to everyone.
  2. (B)The fairness and validity objections to coaching pull against each other, since what strengthens one weakens the other, so critics cannot press both fully.
  3. (C)Exam coaching inflates scores without improving the skills that tests are meant to measure, so it both defeats the purpose of testing and wastes families' money.
  4. (D)Because coaching either builds real skills or teaches mere tricks, both objections to it fail, and critics have no sound reason to oppose coaching at all.

Screening programmes are usually judged by how many cases of disease they find, and by that measure more sensitive tests always look better. But some conditions that screening detects would never have caused harm in a person's lifetime: small, slow-growing abnormalities that would have stayed silent until the person died of something else. Finding these is not a benefit; it converts healthy people into patients, exposing them to anxiety and to treatments with real side effects. Because no one can tell, in an individual case, whether a detected abnormality would have progressed, the overdiagnosed patient is indistinguishable from one whose life was saved, and may well believe that screening saved them. Counting detections therefore overstates what screening achieves, without implying that screening is never worthwhile.

  2. (B)Patients who have been overdiagnosed usually know that their treatment was unnecessary, which makes them suspicious of screening and unwilling to return for tests.

  4. (D)Counting detections overstates screening's value, since some detected conditions would never have caused harm, and those treated for them look just like patients truly helped.

This makes antibiotics unlike most medicines.   4. (D)States drew borders along rivers because, long before railways, river valleys already separated distinct communities that differed in their trade, kinship and speech.

  4. (D)Hypocrisy weakens a critic's authority to demand change but not the correctness of the criticism, and a critic who admits sharing the fault can recover the standing to speak.

Analyses of shooting records found that hits did not cluster more than chance would predict, and the belief was filed alongside other errors of intuition. Later work identified a subtle flaw in the way those records had been analysed: the method used was biased toward finding no streakiness. Corrected analyses suggest that a modest hot hand may exist after all. This does not vindicate the fans entirely, since the effect appears far smaller than players and spectators believe. The episode shows instead that a debunking can itself rest on an error, and that a belief may be exaggerated without being baseless.

On one, guilds were cartels: they restricted entry, fixed prices and kept outsiders from the trade, enriching masters at the expense of customers and would-be rivals. On the other, they were institutions of trust: in markets where buyers could not easily judge the quality of cloth or metalwork, a guild's standards and inspections gave its mark a meaning that no single workshop could establish alone. The two accounts are usually presented as rivals, but they describe mechanisms that can coexist in the same institution, and the more useful question is which one predominated under which conditions. Where quality was easy to observe, the trust function had little to add and restriction was likely to dominate; where quality stayed hidden until long after purchase, the same barriers to entry may have been the price of a reputation worth having.

  3. (C)Where the quality of goods could be judged at a glance, guilds served no useful purpose at all, and towns would have done better without them.
  4. (D)The cartel and trust accounts of guilds are both partly right, because every guild restricted entry to some extent and every guild also inspected its members' goods.

This does not mean that entering first is never valuable; where customers face high costs of switching, an early lead can harden into dominance. CAT-VARC-C10-084 · Para summary · EasyMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

A measurement can be precise without being accurate. If a scale consistently reads two kilograms too heavy, repeated weighings of the same object will agree closely with one another, and that agreement may inspire confidence, yet every reading is wrong by the same amount. Precision describes how tightly repeated results cluster; accuracy describes how close they lie to the true value. The distinction matters because the usual way of checking a result, repeating it, reveals only the first. A systematic error survives any number of repetitions untouched, and can be exposed only by comparing the instrument with an independent standard or a different method. Consistency is therefore reassuring about random noise but silent about bias.

  1. (A)A scale that reads two kilograms too heavy gives readings that agree closely with one another, which is why such scales are trusted more than they deserve.
  2. (B)Scientists should stop repeating measurements and rely only on comparisons with independent standards, since repetition cannot reveal any kind of error.
  3. (C)Precise instruments tend to be inaccurate, because the consistency of their readings conceals errors that less precise instruments would reveal through their scatter.
  4. (D)Repeating a measurement shows whether results agree, not whether they are correct; systematic bias can be exposed only by an independent standard or method.

Batteries are often presented as the answer to the intermittency of wind and solar power: store the surplus when the sun shines and release it when it does not. For smoothing variation over hours they are increasingly effective, absorbing the midday peak of solar output and covering the evening rise in demand. But the harder problem of intermittency is not the daily cycle. It is the windless, overcast week in winter, or the season in which output stays low for months, and covering gaps of that length with batteries of the kind now installed would require storage on a scale that is, at present, very costly. Batteries, then, solve one part of the problem well, and success with that part should not be mistaken for a solution to the whole, which will also need other means such as long-distance transmission, flexible demand or different forms of storage.

Spam filtering differs from most problems of classification in one important respect: the things being classified fight back. A filter that learns to recognise the features of unwanted messages changes the environment it was trained on, because senders whose messages are blocked alter them until they pass. Each improvement therefore tends to erode its own basis, and a filter that performed superbly when it was built may decline without any change to its code. This is why accuracy measured on yesterday's messages is a weak guide to tomorrow's, and why effective filtering is better understood as a continuing contest than as a solved problem. The point is not that filtering is futile, since raising the cost of evasion still reduces what gets through, but that its success has to be renewed rather than achieved once.

  1. (A)Since senders adapt to what filters detect, filtering success decays and must be continually renewed; past accuracy predicts little, though filtering still cuts spam.

  3. (C)Spam filters lose accuracy over time mainly because their code grows outdated as email software and the habits of ordinary users gradually change.
  4. (D)Spam filtering is ultimately futile, since every improvement in detection is soon cancelled out by senders who alter their messages in order to evade it.

A narrator who misleads the reader would seem to undermine the trust on which fiction depends, yet some of the most admired novels are told by narrators whose accounts cannot be taken at face value. The effect works because the author has not deceived the reader in the way the narrator has. Small inconsistencies, the reactions of other characters and gaps between what is said and what is shown allow an attentive reader to reconstruct what the narrator cannot or will not admit. The pleasure lies in that reconstruction, and in watching a mind reveal itself through the very effort of self-justification. An unreliable narrator, in other words, depends on a reliable author: if the clues were absent, the reader would simply be lied to, and the device would collapse into a trick.

  1. (A)An unreliable narrator weakens the trust between author and reader, which is why only a few highly skilled novelists have attempted to use the device.
  2. (B)Novels with unreliable narrators are admired more than other novels because readers enjoy being misled and then surprised by the truth near the end.
  3. (C)Unreliable narration rewards readers because the author plants clues that expose the narrator's distortions; without such clues the device would be mere deception.
  4. (D)Unreliable narrators reveal their characters through self-justification, which makes them more psychologically convincing than the narrators of novels who report events accurately and fully.

Reviews of film adaptations often turn on fidelity: whether the film kept the plot, the characters and the ending of the book. The criterion is natural but confused. A novel can spend pages inside a character's thoughts, stretch a single moment or move freely across decades; a film must render most of this through image, sound and duration, and a scene transferred unchanged may carry quite different weight on screen. Literal faithfulness can therefore betray what made the book matter, while a film that alters events may preserve it. This does not make every departure justified, and some adaptations change a story for no better reason than convenience. But the question worth asking is whether the film achieves in its own medium something comparable to what the book achieved in its, not whether the two can be matched item by item.

  1. (A)Reviewers judge adaptations by their fidelity because a film that keeps the plot, characters and ending of a book generally preserves its significance as well.
  2. (B)Since films cannot convey the inner thoughts that novels describe at length, an adaptation of a novel is bound to lose what made the book worth reading.
  3. (C)Film adaptations should depart freely from their source books, since altering plot and characters is the only way to keep what made the original matter.
  4. (D)Since novels and films work differently, point-by-point fidelity is a poor test of an adaptation; what matters is whether the film achieves something comparable in its own medium.

Improvisation in jazz is often imagined as pure spontaneity, music invented from nothing in the moment of performance. The reality is closer to the opposite. Improvisers spend years absorbing scales, harmonic patterns, phrases from recordings and the habits of other players, until a large vocabulary is available to them without conscious thought. What happens on stage is the fresh combination of this material in response to the other musicians and the shape of the tune, not creation from a blank page. Far from diminishing the achievement, this explains it: spontaneity of the kind audiences admire is possible only because so much has been prepared that the performer is free to listen and respond.

  4. (D)Improvising musicians respond to one another and to the shape of the tune, which is why the same jazz piece sounds different every time it is played.

Those who favour cleaning point out that yellowed varnish distorts colour and flattens depth, so that a viewer sees the grime of centuries rather than the painting. Those who resist it reply that cleaning is irreversible, that it can strip away thin final glazes the artist applied, and that our idea of what the painting originally looked like is itself a guess. Campaigns to protect a language from foreign words usually rest on the fear that borrowing will dilute it or eventually destroy it. The history of languages gives little support to this fear. Most major languages have absorbed large numbers of words from their neighbours, conquerors and trading partners, and they have remained recognisably themselves, because what makes a language distinct lies far more in its grammar and sound system than in the origin of its vocabulary. Borrowed words are typically reshaped to fit the borrowing language's pronunciation and grammatical endings until speakers no longer notice where they came from. Languages do die, but they die when their speakers abandon them for another language, not when they take in words from outside.

Treating them as lesser codes has had practical costs, since it has encouraged schooling that suppressed signing in favour of lip-reading and speech.

The reading moves too quickly.   4. (D)Diversified conglomerates are worth less than their separate divisions because the managers of sprawling firms misallocate capital among businesses they do not understand.

Lists of 'untranslatable' words circulate widely, each entry presented as proof that some culture has grasped a feeling the rest of us cannot even name. The charm of such lists rests on a confusion. That a language has no single word for something does not mean its speakers lack the concept; a phrase of five words can capture what another language packs into one, and the lists themselves translate every entry in a sentence. What is genuinely hard to carry across is different: the associations a word has gathered through use, the contexts where it feels natural, the tone it strikes in a joke or a lament. These are real losses, but they afflict ordinary words as much as exotic ones, and they are a matter of degree rather than an absolute barrier. The lists, by celebrating a handful of curiosities, end up obscuring where the real difficulty of translation lies.

  1. (A)The popular lists of untranslatable words refute themselves, since each of them translates every one of its entries in a sentence of ordinary prose.

  4. (D)Since the associations and tone of words can never be fully carried across, every translation fails absolutely, and lists of untranslatable words merely highlight the most striking cases.

Adding lanes to a jammed motorway looks like the plainest remedy for congestion, yet the relief it brings tends to be short-lived, and a few years on the road is typically as clogged as before. The reason is that congestion had been rationing use of the road. Once journeys speed up, drivers who had been taking other routes switch back, trips postponed to off-peak hours move to the rush hour, some commuters abandon trains for cars, and over a longer span households and employers settle farther apart. The new space is taken up by travel that the jams had been holding down. None of this makes extra capacity worthless, since more people now make journeys they value; it means only that widening is a poor tool for the lasting relief from congestion it is usually sold as delivering.

  1. (A)Widening roads is pointless, since the new lanes are always as clogged as the old ones within a few years and nobody at all benefits from the extra capacity provided.
  2. (B)When roads become faster, people move farther from their jobs, so road widening is the main reason why cities have spread outward into the countryside.
  3. (C)New road capacity tends to fill with trips that congestion had deterred, so widening roads lets more people travel but seldom brings the lasting end to jams it promises.
  4. (D)Traffic jams persist after roads are widened because planners underestimate how quickly a city's population grows, adding new drivers faster than lanes can be built.

It weakens the claim that the old name actively glorifies anyone, but it equally weakens the claim that removing it destroys history, which survives in archives and books rather than on signposts. A census appears to be a passive instrument: the state asks who people are and writes down the answers. But the questions come first, and they are not neutral. To count people by language, religion or ethnicity, an official form must offer a fixed list of boxes, and those boxes rarely match the blurred, overlapping and shifting ways people actually describe themselves. Counting, in short, can help to create the groups it claims merely to record. This does not mean censuses should stop asking such questions, since a state that refuses to count a disadvantaged group may also refuse to see its disadvantage; it means the categories deserve the scrutiny we give to any other policy choice.

  4. (D)Because official categories distort the fluid ways in which people see themselves, governments should stop asking about language, religion and ethnicity in the census.

Before printing, a language written across a wide region existed in many local forms: scribes spelled as they spoke, and a manuscript copied in one town might look foreign in another. Printing changed the economics of this variety. A printer selling thousands of copies wanted a form of the language that readers across the whole market could follow, and once a few presses settled on particular spellings and grammar, every later book that followed them reinforced the choice. Standard written languages owe at least as much to this commercial pressure as to the decrees of scholars or rulers. The standard that emerged was not necessarily the most elegant or the most widely spoken variety; it was often simply the one used where the presses happened to cluster, and the dialects of other regions came to look like departures from a norm that was partly an accident of trade.

  1. (A)Printing wiped out the regional dialects of written languages, because readers refused to buy books written in any variety other than the most widely spoken one.
  2. (B)Printers' need for a form readable across large markets pushed written languages towards a standard that often reflected where presses clustered rather than any special merit of that variety.
  3. (C)Standard written languages were mainly created by scholars and rulers, who chose the most elegant regional varieties and then required printers to adopt them everywhere.
  4. (D)Before printing, scribes spelled words as they pronounced them, so that a manuscript produced in one town could look foreign to readers living in another.

  4. (D)Shared resources need not end in ruin or be handed to markets or states, since rule-bound communities have managed some well, though such arrangements can break down.

When a service becomes cheaper to run, people tend to use more of it: a more economical car makes longer journeys affordable, and cheaper lighting invites more of it. Some of the expected saving is therefore taken back in additional use. It does not follow, as enthusiasts of the idea sometimes conclude, that efficiency is futile. Whether the rebound swallows a small or a large part of the saving depends on how much more of the service people actually want once its price falls, and for many household uses that appetite is limited; nobody keeps the fridge door open because the fridge is economical. The finding is a reason to estimate savings honestly, not a reason to abandon the pursuit of them.

  4. (D)Because cheaper energy services are always used more, gains in efficiency end up raising total consumption, so efforts to save energy through better machines defeat themselves.

One school of critics holds that a poem means what its author intended, and that interpretation is the patient reconstruction of that intention from the text and its circumstances. Another holds that once a work is published its meaning belongs to its readers, so that the author's own account is just one reading among many. Each position captures something the other cannot. The first explains why we can be wrong about a poem, as we are when we take an ironic line for a sincere one; the second explains why a work can say more than its author knew, as when a later age sees in an old novel a pattern its writer never noticed. What neither can do alone is account for both experiences, of being corrected by the text and of being surprised by it, and a defensible view of interpretation will have to make room for both.

  1. (A)The view that a poem means what its author intended is the better supported one, since only the author's intention can explain how a reader comes to misread an ironic line.

  4. (D)Since critics disagree so deeply about where meaning lies, no interpretation of a poem can be judged more correct than another, and every reading stands on equal terms.

Why do people who insist they cannot sing keep turning up, week after week, to rehearse with amateur choirs? Part of the answer lies in what choral singing asks of them. Unlike a solo performance, it does not demand that any one voice be remarkable; it demands that each voice listen to the others and adjust, breathing and entering together. The individual's weaknesses are absorbed into the sound of the group, while the group's sound depends on everyone's attention. Members describe the result less as a musical achievement than as a feeling of belonging that few other activities supply so directly. The music matters, but for many singers it is the occasion for the coordination, and the coordination is what they come back for.

A borrowed word rarely displaces an existing one outright; more often it settles beside it and the two divide the territory, one taking a formal shade and the other an everyday one, so that the language ends up able to say more rather than less. Borrowing is also old: most languages that purists wish to protect were themselves assembled from earlier contacts. None of this means that every borrowing is welcome or that speakers cannot reasonably prefer a native coinage. It means that the decision is one of taste, not a defence against decay.

  4. (D)Borrowed words enrich every language they enter, so efforts by speakers to prefer native coinages over foreign terms are misguided and ought to be abandoned.

A warning that appears everywhere, on products that are mostly harmless as well as on ones that are genuinely dangerous, teaches its readers that it carries little information. A standard textbook argument holds that raising the minimum wage must reduce employment, since employers buy less of anything that becomes more expensive. Comparisons of neighbouring areas where the wage floor rose in one and not the other have often found smaller job losses than that argument predicts, and sometimes none at all. One explanation is that where few employers compete for workers, wages were held below what a competitive market would pay, so a modest floor can raise pay without cutting jobs. It says little about large rises, or about poorer regions where a single national floor sits close to the typical wage, and treating it as proof that minimum wages never cost jobs stretches it beyond what it shows.

  4. (D)Since every guess contains some error, bidders should study the jar more carefully until their estimates of its contents become reliable enough to bid on.

It became common, after a run of supply disruptions, to say that lean manufacturing had been exposed as a mistake: firms that held almost no inventory had saved money in calm years and paid for it many times over when deliveries stopped. The charge contains a truth but misplaces it. Holding little stock was never the whole of the method; its purpose was to make problems visible quickly so that they could be fixed, and the firms that practised it most seriously also cultivated close, long relationships with a few suppliers and knew in detail where their parts came from. What failed in many cases was a cheaper imitation: low inventory adopted as a cost target, combined with suppliers chosen solely on price and scattered across distant regions that nobody had mapped. The lesson, then, is less that buffers are always wise than that thin buffers are safe only where the knowledge and relationships the method assumed are actually present.

  4. (D)The disruptions discredit less lean production itself than a version that cut stock while neglecting the supplier knowledge and ties on which thin inventories safely depend.

A new instrument, made for some modest practical purpose, reveals phenomena that no existing theory anticipated, and theory then scrambles to absorb them; the instrument, in effect, sets the agenda. Yet grouping by age assumes that children born within the same twelve months are ready for the same material at the same moment, an assumption that every experienced teacher knows to be false. In classrooms that deliberately mix ages, the older children consolidate what they know by explaining it, and the younger ones see, in the work of those just ahead of them, what the next step looks like.
