A familiar remedy for textbooks that students find hard is to rewrite them in shorter sentences with commoner words, guided by formulas that score a text by the length of its words and sentences. The formulas do measure something, but what makes a passage hard to understand is often not its vocabulary so much as the gaps it leaves for the reader to fill. Cutting one long sentence into three short ones frequently removes the very words, such as because, although and therefore, that told the reader how the ideas were related; the result scores as easier and is harder to follow. None of this means that dense prose is a virtue or that difficult words never obstruct learning. It means that a readability score measures surface features, and a text revised to improve its score can lose the connective tissue on which comprehension depends.

  1. (A)Short sentences make textbooks harder to follow than long ones, so formulas that reward brevity measure the opposite of what makes a text truly difficult.
  2. (B)Readability formulas are worthless guides for textbook writers, since the length of the words and sentences in a passage has no bearing at all on how easily students can understand what it says.
  3. (C)Textbooks should be written in long and complex sentences, since students learn more from dense prose that forces them to work out the meaning for themselves.
  4. (D)Revising textbooks to raise readability scores can backfire, because the scores track surface features, and shortening sentences may strip out the links between ideas that understanding needs.

Critics of everyday courtesy often complain that phrases such as 'how are you' or 'thank you so much' are empty, since the speaker rarely wants a medical report and is seldom overwhelmed with gratitude. The complaint assumes that words are sincere only when they report an inner state. But many polite formulas are not reports at all; they are moves in a shared routine, closer to a handshake than to a confession. Their meaning lies in being offered and returned, and their omission is noticed precisely because nobody expects them to carry personal feeling. To call them insincere is therefore to judge them by a standard they never claimed to meet. This does not mean that every courteous word is innocent: a formula can be used to hold someone at a distance or to cover contempt. Yet that misuse is possible only because the routine itself has a recognised social meaning.

  4. (D)No courteous expression can ever be insincere, since the meaning of every polite phrase lies wholly in social routine and never in the feelings of the speaker.

It is tempting to think of the rules of a game as fences around an activity that exists without them, but moving a bishop diagonally is not a restriction on some freer way of moving a carved piece; outside the rules there is no bishop to move. Rules in this sense constitute a game rather than regulate it. From this some infer that whatever the rules permit is fair, and that a player who finds a loophole has simply played well. The inference is too quick. Rules are written to make a particular contest possible, and a move that satisfies their letter while dissolving that contest, such as a team deliberately losing to secure an easier opponent later, keeps the form of the game while emptying it. Still, the spirit of a game is no free-floating ideal that referees may invoke at will; it can only be read from the contest the rules were built to create, which is why the usual remedy for such an exploit is a better-drafted rule rather than an appeal to conscience.

  1. (A)Teams that lose on purpose to obtain an easier opponent later break no rule, but their conduct shows a lack of respect for the opponents they face in that match.
  2. (B)Rules constitute a game, yet obeying their letter does not settle fairness, since a legal move can empty the contest, though the game's spirit is itself read from the rules.
  3. (C)Because written rules can always be exploited, referees should judge each contest by the spirit of the game and set aside the letter of the rules when the two conflict.
  4. (D)Since rules constitute a game instead of restricting it, any move the written rules permit counts as fair play, and exploiting a loophole is simply a sign of skill.

Where meetings start exactly on the hour, time is treated as a resource divided among commitments, and lateness signals that one has taken someone else's share. Each system is strict, but about different things.   4. (D)Loose attitudes to time reflect a deeper respect for people, which makes relationship-bound societies kinder and more humane than those organised strictly by the clock.

The history is less tidy. There is some truth in this: texts that nobody valued were seldom recopied, and a text not recopied every few generations was likely to perish with its material. Works survived when they suited the needs of schools and religious communities, when they were short enough to copy cheaply, or when they had been gathered into anthologies that travelled together; a brilliant treatise in an unfashionable field could vanish while a mediocre school text was copied hundreds of times. The surviving corpus is a record of what later centuries used, and our sense of what the ancients thought best is partly an inheritance of those uses.

Trees of the same species growing in the same region therefore share a pattern of wide and narrow rings, much like a barcode written by the weather. The question sounds sensible but can mislead badly. In arid places the average is a statistical fiction that few actual years resemble: runs of meagre seasons are broken by an occasional deluge, and the mean sits between them, describing no year that anyone lives through. Herders who move their animals in search of rain and keep flexible claims to distant pasture are responding to this variability, not ignoring good practice, while schemes that settle them on plots sized for an average year can fail in the first drought. The convention turns the novel into a contest between writer and reader, and much of the genre's pleasure comes from losing that contest fairly. But fairness has a price. 'A child could have painted that' is the oldest complaint against abstract art, and defenders usually reply that the painter could also draw a perfect likeness and chose not to. The reply concedes too much. It accepts the critic's assumption that a picture is valuable in proportion to the difficulty of making it, and merely insists that the difficulty lies elsewhere, in the years of training behind the apparent simplicity. But much of what matters in such work is not difficulty at all; it is the decision about what to leave out, the sense of when a canvas is finished, the placing of a single shape so that it holds a whole surface. Publishers often keep the translator's name off the cover of a translated novel, on the theory that readers want to feel they are meeting the author directly and that a second name reminds them of an intermediary. The reasoning is understandable, but it rests on a fiction. Every sentence the reader of a translation encounters was chosen by the translator: the rhythm, the register, the jokes that land or fall flat. To hide that person is not to remove the intermediary but to conceal one who is doing a great deal of the work, and the concealment has costs. Naming the translator does not spoil the illusion of direct contact so much as make the reader's trust an informed one.

  4. (D)Because their names are hidden on covers, translators struggle to build reputations and so cannot command the fees and the projects that their considerable work on each novel deserves.

Critics of sampling in popular music often argue that a producer who builds a track from fragments of other people's recordings is not composing at all but merely assembling, and that the result borrows its value from the originals. The argument assumes that originality lies in producing sounds no one has produced before. What distinguishes a composer is not the novelty of the materials but what is done with them, the choices of selection, placement and transformation that make a familiar element mean something new. This does not settle whether any particular sampled track is good, or whether the original performers deserve payment; those are separate questions. It shows only that sampling cannot be dismissed as uncreative merely because its raw materials already exist.

  3. (C)Because composers in every period have borrowed scales, forms and melodies, no music has ever been truly original, and critics should abandon the idea of originality altogether.
  4. (D)The charge that sampling is uncreative locates originality in new materials, a test most music would fail; creativity lies in transforming materials, though this leaves a track's quality and payment unsettled.

Style manuals routinely tell writers to avoid the passive voice, and the advice has hardened into a rule that many writers apply without thinking. The case against the passive is real but narrow. A passive sentence can hide the agent ('mistakes were made'), and a long run of passives can make prose feel inert. But the passive also does work that nothing else does as neatly. It lets a writer keep the topic of a paragraph in the subject position, so that a passage about a disease can go on talking about the disease ('it is spread by mosquitoes') rather than lurching to whatever happens to cause it. It lets scientists foreground a procedure rather than the person who carried it out, when the person is irrelevant. The useful question is never whether a sentence is passive but whether the reader needs to know who acted, and what the sentence should be about.

  3. (C)Writers should generally prefer the passive voice to the active, because it keeps the topic of a passage in subject position and directs attention to procedures rather than people.
  4. (D)The passive voice serves mainly to conceal responsibility, as in 'mistakes were made', and style manuals are right to warn writers against using it in serious prose.

Whether listening to an audiobook counts as reading is usually argued as a question of status, with defenders insisting that the story reaches the mind either way and sceptics treating listening as a lesser substitute. Both sides tend to miss what actually differs. For narrative of moderate difficulty, a competent listener appears to follow and remember the story about as well as a reader does, so the sceptic's claim that listening is shallower is hard to sustain as a general rule. The difference lies in control. A reader sets the pace, slows down at a difficult sentence, glances back to check who said what, and lets the eye run ahead; a listener receives the text at the narrator's speed and must make an effort to go back. For a thriller this hardly matters. For a dense argument, where comprehension depends on rereading, it may matter a great deal. The useful question is therefore not whether listening is reading, but what kind of text is being taken in.

  1. (A)Listening is not generally shallower than reading, but it gives less control over pace and going back, a difference that matters little for stories and possibly much for dense argument.
  2. (B)Audiobooks are an inferior substitute for printed books, since listeners cannot slow down or glance back, and so they understand and remember less of whatever they hear.
  3. (C)Readers and listeners differ mainly in speed, since narrators read faster than most people, so audiobooks suit thrillers better than slow and descriptive literary fiction.
  4. (D)Listening to an audiobook is a form of reading, since the story reaches a listener's mind just as it reaches a reader's, so the debate about its status is pointless.

  4. (D)Text messaging improves children's spelling, because abbreviating words playfully requires them to know the full spellings, and the habit keeps reinforcing that knowledge every day.

Impartial moral theories hold that everyone's interests count equally, and they seem to imply that I should be no more concerned with my friend's welfare than with a stranger's. Critics reply that a person who really thought this way would be incapable of friendship, since to be someone's friend just is to give their good special weight. The critics are right that friendship involves partiality, but the impartialist need not concede that partiality is therefore unjustified. One can argue impartially that a world in which people form close attachments and favour those they love is better for everyone than a world of detached benefactors, and so endorse partiality at the level of the practice while denying that each act of favouritism must be weighed against strangers' claims. The cost of this reply is that the friend's special claim is defended, in the end, by appeal to the general good, which some will feel misdescribes why we help our friends.

  1. (A)Impartial theories require people to weigh every act of favouritism toward a friend against the claims of strangers, which makes genuine friendship impossible to sustain.
  2. (B)Impartialists can grant that friendship is partial by justifying, on impartial grounds, the practice of favouring loved ones, though grounding a friend's claim in the general good may misdescribe it.
  3. (C)Because friendship requires giving a friend's good special weight, impartial moral theories cannot be correct, and morality must permit each person to favour those they love.
  4. (D)A world of close attachments is better for everyone than a world of detached benefactors, so people have no reason at all to be concerned with the welfare of strangers.

It is often said that a gift made anonymously is morally superior to one made publicly, since the anonymous donor cannot be seeking praise and so must be acting from pure generosity. The reasoning confuses evidence with value. Anonymity can be good evidence that a donor is not after recognition, but a public donor is not thereby shown to be after it, and even a donor who enjoys the praise may give for reasons that are mostly good. More importantly, the recipients are fed and housed just the same either way, and a well-publicised gift may persuade others to give, multiplying its effect. There may be cases where anonymity matters, as when publicity would humiliate the person helped. But as a general ranking, the preference for anonymous giving judges a gift by what it reveals about the giver rather than by what it does.

  1. (A)Anonymous giving is preferable only when publicity would humiliate the people being helped, which is the one case where the donor's motive really matters morally.
  2. (B)Donors who give anonymously are no more generous than those who give publicly, since everyone who gives to charity enjoys receiving at least some praise for doing so.
  3. (C)Ranking anonymous gifts higher confuses evidence of motive with a gift's worth; public gifts help recipients equally and may inspire others, though anonymity sometimes protects recipients.
  4. (D)Public gifts are morally better than anonymous ones, because they encourage other people to give and so multiply the good that each donation does for those in need.

Moral rules such as 'do not lie' or 'keep your promises' are often attacked by pointing to exceptions: surely one may lie to save a life, or break a trivial promise to help someone in danger. If a rule has exceptions, the argument runs, it was never really a rule, and we should simply weigh consequences case by case. But the existence of exceptions shows less than this suggests. A rule can be a strong presumption, one that sets the burden of argument and holds unless something weighty overrides it, and such presumptions are valuable precisely because case-by-case weighing is slow, prone to self-serving bias and impossible for others to rely on. Someone who keeps promises only when calculation favours it is not a person others can plan around. The exceptions show that rules are not absolute; they do not show that rules are dispensable.

  1. (A)Exceptions show only that moral rules are not absolute, not that they are dispensable, since as strong presumptions they guard against slow, self-serving case-by-case judgement and make people reliable.
  2. (B)Case-by-case moral judgement is always self-serving, so people should keep to moral rules even in emergencies where breaking a rule would save a life.
  3. (C)Moral rules such as keeping promises must be followed without exception, because a person who breaks one even once to help someone can never again be relied on by others.
  4. (D)Since one may lie to save a life, moral rules are really summaries of past calculations, and in each new case we should weigh the consequences afresh.

But the transforming power of consent depends on the conditions under which it is given. A worker who accepts dangerous conditions because the alternative is destitution has agreed, in a sense, and it would be odd to say his signature means nothing. To treat such consent as settling the matter is to let the stronger party borrow the moral authority of a free choice that was barely available.   4. (D)Employers who offer dangerous work to desperate people act wrongly, and the state should set minimum safety standards that no contract between them may lower.

The view has real appeal, since it explains why amnesia seems to threaten the self in a way that losing a limb does not. But it struggles with ordinary forgetting. Critics of paternalism usually frame the issue as a contest between welfare and freedom: the state may make you better off, but only by overriding your choices. This framing concedes too much to the paternalist and too little to the person. It assumes that we already know what is good for someone independently of what they would choose, so that the only question is whether to impose it. In many cases we do not. Whether a risky career, a strict diet or a dangerous hobby serves a person's welfare depends partly on the plans and commitments that give their life its shape, and these are often visible only in their choices. Respecting someone's decisions is therefore not merely a price paid for liberty; it is frequently our best evidence of what their welfare actually is.

  4. (D)Paternalism forces a hard choice between making people better off and leaving them free, and its critics rightly hold that freedom should usually win that contest.

It is sometimes argued that a country which can produce everything more efficiently than its neighbours has nothing to gain from trading with them. The intuition is natural and wrong. What matters for trade is not absolute efficiency but relative cost: what a country gives up to make one good instead of another. Even a country that is better at everything will be much better at some things than at others, and it gains by concentrating on those and buying the rest. The argument does not, however, promise that everyone within each country gains; workers in the industries that shrink may lose, and whether they are compensated is a political question that the logic of trade does not settle.

  1. (A)Trade benefits even a country that is better at everything, because gains depend on relative rather than absolute cost, though this does not ensure that every group inside it benefits.
  2. (B)A country able to make every good more efficiently than its neighbours has little reason to trade with them, since it can meet all of its own needs more cheaply.
  3. (C)Even a country that is better at everything gains from trade, so opening its markets benefits its citizens as long as they concentrate on what they do best.
  4. (D)Because workers in shrinking industries lose from trade, governments are obliged to compensate them fully before opening their home markets to more efficient foreign producers.

A household that borrowed a fixed sum to buy a house repays the loan in money that is worth less each year, so unexpected inflation quietly transfers wealth from the lender to the borrower. What matters is not just how fast prices rise but whether the rise was foreseen; lenders who expect inflation can demand higher interest in advance, and the transfer then largely disappears.

In practice, no theory yields a prediction by itself; every test also relies on assumptions about the instruments, the experimental conditions and other theories taken for granted. When the prediction fails, logic tells us only that something in this bundle is wrong, not which part. Scientists who keep a theory after a failed test and blame an auxiliary assumption are therefore not necessarily being dogmatic; sometimes the faulty instrument really is the culprit. What separates a reasonable rescue from an evasive one is whether the blamed assumption can be checked independently, rather than being invoked only to save the theory.

  4. (D)When a prediction fails, scientists should suspect their instruments first, since faulty equipment rather than the theory itself is usually to blame for the failure.

Seismologists distinguish between predicting an earthquake and forecasting one, and public discussion often blurs the two. A prediction would name a place, a window of days or weeks and a magnitude, precisely enough to justify evacuating a city. No method has reliably done this, and claims to the contrary have tended to collapse under scrutiny. A forecast is more modest: it estimates the probability of a strong quake in a region over years or decades, using the record of past ruptures and the strain measured on faults. A forecast cannot tell anyone to leave on Tuesday, but it can tell a city how strict its building codes should be and where a hospital ought not to be built. Judged as failed predictions, forecasts look useless; judged as what they are, they are among the most practically valuable things the science produces.

  3. (C)Better measurement of the strain on faults will soon let seismologists turn their long-range forecasts into precise predictions that justify evacuating whole cities.
  4. (D)Since seismologists cannot say when an earthquake will strike, their probability estimates are of little practical use and should not be allowed to guide how cities are planned or where they build.

  4. (D)Zoos are justified chiefly by their breeding programmes, which have kept alive species that vanished in the wild and have allowed several of them to be returned to their habitats.

Voice notes sit awkwardly between a phone call and a text, and both their appeal and their irritation come from that position. For the sender, a voice note combines the ease of talking with the freedom of a text: there is no need to type, and no need to catch the other person at a convenient moment. Both camps miss what hand calculation was quietly teaching. A child who has worked many sums develops a sense of size, so that an answer of four thousand to a problem about the price of a school lunch looks absurd at once. That sense is what catches the mistyped digit, and a calculator, which never doubts itself, cannot supply it. The point is not that the machines should be banned, but that estimation must be taught on purpose once the drills that used to teach it by accident are gone.

  1. (A)Children who rely on calculators make more errors than those who work sums by hand, because the machines frequently produce answers that are simply wrong.
  2. (B)Hand calculation built a feel for the size of answers that catches errors, so if calculators replace the drills, estimation has to be taught deliberately rather than left to chance.
  3. (C)Calculators free children from the drudgery of long division and let them spend their time on the real mathematics that endless drills used to crowd out.
  4. (D)Calculators should be kept out of primary classrooms altogether, because only children who work long sums by hand ever develop the sense of size needed to catch their own mistakes.
CAT-VARC-C10-343 · Para summary · DifficultMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

Student ratings of teaching are often dismissed as popularity contests, and there is something in the charge: ratings tend to rise when courses are easier and grades kinder, and they can reflect a lecturer's charm as much as what students learn. Yet throwing the ratings out entirely would discard information that no one else possesses. Only students sit through every class; only they know whether the lecturer arrived prepared, returned work on time or answered questions clearly. The sensible course is to ask students about what they observed rather than what they judge. 'Were assignments returned within two weeks?' is a question they can answer reliably; 'Was this a good teacher?' invites them to rate something they are not well placed to assess.

  1. (A)Student ratings measure popularity rather than learning, since they rise for easy courses and charming lecturers, and so universities would do better to abandon them altogether.
  2. (B)Students are poor judges even of whether lecturers prepare well or return work on time, so their ratings should always be checked against regular classroom visits by experienced colleagues.
  3. (C)Because only students sit through every class, their verdict on whether a lecturer is a good teacher is more reliable than the verdict of any other observer.
  4. (D)Student ratings are unreliable as overall verdicts on teaching, but students alone witness everyday classroom conduct, so surveys should ask what they observed rather than how good the teacher was.

The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

When universities began recording lectures and posting them online, many lecturers feared that their halls would empty, and in some courses attendance did fall. Yet the way students tend to use the recordings is easy to overlook. They seldom sit through a whole lecture a second time; instead they return to the stretch that confused them, pause on a derivation, or replay a definition on the night before an examination. For a student who missed a class through illness or a part-time job, the recording is a lifeline. The loss of attendance is real, and a lecture watched alone offers no chance to put up a hand and ask. But the recordings mostly serve as a reference to be consulted rather than a substitute for being there.

  1. (A)For students who miss a class because of illness or a part-time job, a recording of the lecture is the only way of keeping up with the course.
  2. (B)Recorded lectures have largely emptied university halls, since students now prefer to watch at home, where they can pause and replay every lecture whenever they like.
  3. (C)Recorded lectures may cut attendance somewhat, but students mostly use them to revisit hard passages or catch up on missed classes, as a reference rather than a replacement.
  4. (D)Students who rely on recorded lectures learn more than those who attend in person, because they can pause on a derivation and replay a definition before an examination.
CAT-VARC-C10-345 · Para summary · MediumMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

Reading aloud is usually treated as something adults do for small children and stop once the children can read for themselves. That timing assumes that the only purpose of listening to a story is to gain access to words one cannot yet decode. But a teenager who reads fluently still gains from hearing a skilled reader. Spoken aloud, a difficult text arrives with its phrasing, pauses and emphasis already worked out, so that sentences which would stall a silent reader become easy to follow, and the listener meets books a little beyond what they would attempt alone. Reading aloud to older pupils is therefore not a step backwards. It serves a different purpose from the bedtime story, and that purpose does not expire once decoding is mastered.

  1. (A)Secondary teachers should replace most silent reading with reading aloud, since pupils understand far more of a text when they hear it than when they read it.
  2. (B)Teenagers who are read to aloud fall behind in reading on their own, since hearing texts spoken stops them from learning to work through difficult sentences unaided.
  3. (C)Reading aloud is mainly a way of helping children who cannot yet decode words, and adults are right to stop doing it once children can read by themselves.
  4. (D)Reading aloud still helps adolescents who read fluently, because a skilled performance makes demanding texts accessible, a purpose quite separate from helping young children decode words.
CAT-VARC-C10-346 · Para summary · MediumMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

It is natural to treat a fever as the illness itself and to bring it down as quickly as possible. Yet fever is in large part a response that the body mounts against infection, and a raised temperature can make conditions harder for some invading organisms while speeding parts of the immune response. This does not mean that fevers should be left alone. Very high temperatures can be dangerous, and in young children, the elderly or people with heart disease the strain of a fever may matter more than any benefit it brings. For most otherwise healthy adults, though, the main reason to take a fever-reducing drug is comfort, and it is worth knowing that one is treating a sign of the fight rather than the enemy.

  1. (A)Because a raised temperature speeds the immune response, people who run high fevers tend to recover faster than those whose temperatures stay near normal.
  2. (B)Fever is largely the body's defence against infection rather than the illness, so although it sometimes needs lowering, bringing it down in healthy adults mainly relieves discomfort.
  3. (C)Fever-reducing drugs are dangerous for young children, the elderly and people with heart disease, who should therefore have their fevers treated by other means.
  4. (D)Because fever helps the body fight infection, people should avoid fever-reducing drugs altogether and simply let a raised temperature run its full course in almost every case of illness.
CAT-VARC-C10-347 · Para summary · DifficultMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

Asking patients to rate their pain from zero to ten seems a sensible way of bringing a private experience into the clinic, and as a way of following one patient over time it often works: a drop from eight to four after a drug means something. The trouble begins when the numbers are compared across people or treated as targets. One patient's six is another's three, shaped by temperament, culture and past experience of pain, and a ward that aims to bring every score below a set threshold may end up dosing the number rather than the person. The scale is a ruler each patient carries privately; it can measure change in the one who holds it, but it was never calibrated against anyone else's.

  1. (A)A zero-to-ten pain rating can track change within one patient, but since each person's scale is private, comparing scores across patients or using them as targets misleads.
  2. (B)Because pain is a private experience, no number a patient reports can tell a clinician anything meaningful, not even how that patient's own pain has changed.
  3. (C)Wards that aim to bring every pain score below a fixed level tend to overprescribe painkillers, because patients exaggerate their scores in order to receive more.
  4. (D)Numeric pain scales are unreliable because patients rate pain according to temperament and culture, so clinicians should trust their own judgement of how much pain a patient feels.
CAT-VARC-C10-348 · Para summary · DifficultMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

Drug trials often measure a surrogate, such as blood pressure or a level in the blood, instead of the outcome patients care about, such as living longer or avoiding a stroke. The practice is defensible: real outcomes may take years to appear, and a surrogate that reliably tracks them lets useful drugs reach patients sooner. But the reliability is a property of the link, not of the marker. A drug may improve the number by a route that has nothing to do with the disease, or improve it while causing harm elsewhere, so that a marker which tracked outcomes well for one class of drugs misleads for another. A surrogate is a promise made by past evidence about one mechanism, and each new kind of treatment has to earn the right to rely on it.

  1. (A)Drug trials should measure only the outcomes patients actually care about, such as survival, since markers like blood pressure have proved too unreliable to use.
  2. (B)Surrogate markers are acceptable in drug trials only when the real outcomes they stand for would take many years to appear and when patients urgently need new treatments for a serious disease.
  3. (C)Surrogate markers can legitimately speed trials, but since their link to real outcomes holds only for particular mechanisms, a new kind of drug that improves a marker cannot be assumed to help.
  4. (D)Drugs that improve surrogate markers usually cause harm elsewhere in the body, which is why their apparent benefits so often fail to show up in real outcomes.
CAT-VARC-C10-349 · Para summary · EasyMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

A video consultation and a visit to the clinic are often compared as if one simply replaced the other, but each suits different needs. For a follow-up on a known condition, a review of test results or a question about medication, the video call saves the patient a journey and a waiting room, and loses little. For a new symptom, the screen is a poorer instrument: a doctor cannot press an abdomen, listen to a chest or notice the small signs a patient does not think to mention. The useful question is therefore not which format is better, but which encounters each one serves well.

  1. (A)Doctors cannot examine patients properly over a video link, so video consultations are an unsafe substitute for the clinic and should generally be avoided.
  2. (B)During a video call a doctor cannot press an abdomen or listen to a chest, and may miss small signs that a patient does not think to mention.
  3. (C)Video consultations are more convenient than clinic visits in every respect and will in time replace face-to-face care for almost all patients, whatever their condition or symptoms.
  4. (D)Video calls suit routine follow-ups and clinic visits suit new symptoms needing examination, so the real question is which encounters each format serves, not which is better.
CAT-VARC-C10-350 · Para summary · MediumMCQ
The passage given below is followed by four summaries. Choose the option that best captures the essence of the passage.

Clinical guidelines are sometimes resented as cookbook medicine, a set of rules that turns the doctor into a technician. The resentment misunderstands what a good guideline is. It condenses the evidence on how a typical patient with a given condition fares under different treatments, and for that typical patient it will usually beat the unaided judgement of a busy clinician. But no patient is exactly typical. A guideline drawn from trials that excluded the very old, or people with several illnesses at once, says little about the frail patient with four conditions in the consulting room. Following it there is not rigour but a misreading of its scope. The skill that guidelines demand is knowing when a patient falls inside them.

  1. (A)Guidelines are useless for elderly patients and for those with several illnesses, who should never be treated according to the evidence from clinical trials.
  2. (B)Guidelines distil evidence that usually serves typical patients better than unaided judgement, but they must be applied with care over whether a given patient resembles those the evidence covered.
  3. (C)Clinical guidelines reduce doctors to technicians following rules, and good doctors should rely on their own judgement rather than on summaries of trial evidence.
  4. (D)Because no patient is exactly typical, doctors should treat clinical guidelines only as rough suggestions and depart from them whenever their own judgement of a case happens to differ.
